I mean, I get frustrated with myself when I lose track, but I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless or that I’ve stopped enjoying things. At least, that’s not what I’ve really noticed. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. I guess what finally pushed me to come in is… it’s been harder than I’d like to admit to stay with things at work. Like I’ll sit down to start an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting fully intending to follow along, and within a few minutes I realize my mind has wandered. Then I’m rereading the same numbers over and over, or I suddenly notice I’ve missed the main point of what was just discussed.\n\nI can usually cover for it, I think, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should probably talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: When your mind wanders during these tasks, do you find yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear way. I mean, I get frustrated with myself when I lose track, but I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless or that I’ve stopped enjoying things. At least, that’s not what I’ve really noticed. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve had trouble staying focused on tasks or felt restless, even before these work-related challenges started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, because I did fine in school overall, but even in elementary school I remember teachers saying things like I was bright but careless. My mom used to have to sit with me to get homework finished because I’d sort of lose track of what I was supposed to be doing, or I’d forget a book at school and then couldn’t do the assignment properly.\n\nSo, yes, I guess it was there before work. I just maybe learned to work around it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When you were growing up, did you often feel restless, fidgety, or have trouble staying seated, especially when you were expected to be still or quiet?\n\nPatient: Not especially, I don’t think. I wasn’t the kid who was always out of her seat or getting in trouble for being too noisy. I mean, maybe I doodled or shifted around a little, like any child, but I don’t remember restlessness being the main issue.\n\nIt was more… well, I’m not sure if this is answering what you’re asking, but I don’t have a strong memory of being physically unable to sit still. Does that sound useful?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any situations recently where you found it hard to stay focused, even when the task was something you found interesting or important?\n\nPatient: Yes… unfortunately, I think that’s exactly what’s been happening. I’ll sit down to work on an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting, and I really do intend to follow it. It’s important, and I’m not trying to tune out.\n\nBut then a few minutes in, I realize my mind has gone somewhere else, and I’m rereading the same numbers over and over, or I suddenly notice I’ve missed the point of what someone was saying. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because these are things I should be able to track. I don’t know if that’s the kind of example you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed similar difficulties staying focused in other areas of your life, like at home or during social interactions, or does this mostly happen at work?\n\nPatient: I notice it most at work, I think. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you feel down or sad most of the time, even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not most of the time. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly strenuous?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially after a long workday, but I haven’t really noticed feeling wiped out or low-energy when I haven’t done much.\n\nAt least nothing that stood out to me as a pattern. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you often feel overwhelmed by tasks that require planning or organization, or if you find yourself starting projects without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I often start projects without thinking them through. I usually try to be pretty responsible about that, maybe because of my job.\n\nThe planning and organization part… I guess some tasks can feel bigger in my head than they probably should, but I’m not sure I’d call it overwhelmed exactly. It may depend on the task. If there are a lot of pieces to line up, I can feel a little stuck getting started, but I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of times when you’ve acted quickly without thinking things through, like saying something you later regretted or making a decision without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’m sure I’ve had moments where I answered too quickly or wished I’d phrased something differently, like anyone, but I can’t think of a pattern of making impulsive decisions or saying things that cause real problems.\n\nI try to be pretty careful, especially at work. If anything, I probably think things over too much sometimes. Is that helpful, or am I being too narrow with the question?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know if that’s the kind of example you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed similar difficulties staying focused in other areas of your life, like at home or during social interactions, or does this mostly happen at work?\n\nPatient: I notice it most at work, I think. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart everywhere, because I’m not, but work is where it’s become hard to ignore.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I’ll start an EPIC quality report or sit down in a clinic metrics meeting with every intention of following it, and then within a few minutes my mind has drifted so far that I’m rereading the same numbers or realizing I missed the whole point of the discussion.\n\nThat’s been embarrassing, because these are important things. I’m not sure if I’ve noticed it as clearly at home or socially, or maybe the consequences just aren’t as obvious there. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you feel down or sad most of the time, even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not most of the time. I mean, I have frustrating days, and I can feel discouraged when I’m not keeping up the way I think I should, but I wouldn’t describe it as being down or sad for no clear reason most of the day.\n\nAt least, I haven’t really noticed a pattern like that. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly strenuous?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially after a long workday, but I haven’t really noticed feeling wiped out or low-energy when I haven’t done much.\n\nAt least nothing that stood out to me as a pattern. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you often feel overwhelmed by tasks that require planning or organization, or if you find yourself starting projects without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I often start projects without thinking them through. I usually try to be pretty responsible about that, maybe because of my job.\n\nThe planning and organization part… I guess some tasks can feel bigger in my head than they probably should, but I’m not sure I’d call it overwhelmed exactly. It may depend on the task. If there are a lot of pieces to line up, I can feel a little stuck getting started, but I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of times when you’ve acted quickly without thinking things through, like saying something you later regretted or making a decision without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’m sure I’ve had moments where I answered too quickly or wished I’d phrased something differently, like anyone, but I can’t think of a pattern of making impulsive decisions or saying things that cause real problems.\n\nI try to be pretty careful, especially at work. If anything, I probably think things over too much sometimes. Is that helpful, or am I being too narrow with the question?\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you feel less interested in things you usually care about, or where you don’t get as much pleasure out of activities as you normally would?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way that really stands out. I mean, everyone has off days where you’re tired or just not as excited about something, but I haven’t noticed that I’m not enjoying things I normally care about.\n\nAt least, nothing that feels like a pattern. I still care about my usual activities and responsibilities. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you feel down or sad most of the time, even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not most of the time. I mean, I have frustrating days, and I can feel discouraged when I’m not keeping up the way I think I should, but I wouldn’t describe it as being down or sad for no clear reason most of the day.\n\nAt least, I haven’t really noticed a pattern like that. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly strenuous?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially after a long workday, but I haven’t really noticed feeling wiped out or low-energy when I haven’t done much.\n\nAt least nothing that stood out to me as a pattern. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you often feel overwhelmed by tasks that require planning or organization, or if you find yourself starting projects without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I often start projects without thinking them through. I usually try to be pretty responsible about that, maybe because of my job.\n\nThe planning and organization part… I guess some tasks can feel bigger in my head than they probably should, but I’m not sure I’d call it overwhelmed exactly. It may depend on the task. If there are a lot of pieces to line up, I can feel a little stuck getting started, but I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of times when you’ve acted quickly without thinking things through, like saying something you later regretted or making a decision without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’m sure I’ve had moments where I answered too quickly or wished I’d phrased something differently, like anyone, but I can’t think of a pattern of making impulsive decisions or saying things that cause real problems.\n\nI try to be pretty careful, especially at work. If anything, I probably think things over too much sometimes. Is that helpful, or am I being too narrow with the question?\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you feel less interested in things you usually care about, or where you don’t get as much pleasure out of activities as you normally would?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way that really stands out. I mean, everyone has off days where you’re tired or just not as excited about something, but I haven’t noticed that I’m not enjoying things I normally care about.\n\nAt least, nothing that feels like a pattern. I still care about my usual activities and responsibilities. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situations outside of work, like when you’re reading, watching something, or talking with friends, where you’ve had trouble staying focused or found your mind wandering a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work it can happen, but honestly the thing that pushed me to come in is more at work.\n\nLike, I’ll start an EPIC quality report or sit down in a clinic metrics meeting really meaning to follow everything, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has gone somewhere else entirely. I’m rereading the same numbers, or I suddenly realize I missed the main point of what someone was saying.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m not trying, because I am. It’s just… that drifting happens more than I’d like. Is that along the lines of what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure if I’ve noticed it as clearly at home or socially, or maybe the consequences just aren’t as obvious there. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you feel down or sad most of the time, even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not most of the time. I mean, I have frustrating days, and I can feel discouraged when I’m not keeping up the way I think I should, but I wouldn’t describe it as being down or sad for no clear reason most of the day.\n\nAt least, I haven’t really noticed a pattern like that. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly strenuous?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially after a long workday, but I haven’t really noticed feeling wiped out or low-energy when I haven’t done much.\n\nAt least nothing that stood out to me as a pattern. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you often feel overwhelmed by tasks that require planning or organization, or if you find yourself starting projects without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I often start projects without thinking them through. I usually try to be pretty responsible about that, maybe because of my job.\n\nThe planning and organization part… I guess some tasks can feel bigger in my head than they probably should, but I’m not sure I’d call it overwhelmed exactly. It may depend on the task. If there are a lot of pieces to line up, I can feel a little stuck getting started, but I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of times when you’ve acted quickly without thinking things through, like saying something you later regretted or making a decision without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. If anything, I probably think things over too much sometimes. Is that helpful, or am I being too narrow with the question?\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you feel less interested in things you usually care about, or where you don’t get as much pleasure out of activities as you normally would?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way that really stands out. I still care about my usual activities and responsibilities. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situations outside of work, like when you’re reading, watching something, or talking with friends, where you’ve had trouble staying focused or found your mind wandering a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work it can happen, but honestly the thing that pushed me to come in is more at work.\n\nLike, I’ll start an EPIC quality report or sit down in a clinic metrics meeting really meaning to follow everything, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has gone somewhere else entirely. I just maybe learned to work around it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: When you were growing up, did you often feel restless, fidgety, or have trouble staying seated, especially when you were expected to be still or quiet?\n\nPatient: Not especially, I don’t think. I wasn’t the kid who was always out of her seat or getting in trouble for being too noisy. I mean, maybe I doodled or shifted around a little, like any child, but I don’t remember restlessness being the main issue.\n\nIt was more… well, I’m not sure if this is answering what you’re asking, but I don’t have a strong memory of being physically unable to sit still. Does that sound useful?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any situations recently where you found it hard to stay focused, even when the task was something you found interesting or important?\n\nPatient: Yes… unfortunately, I think that’s exactly what’s been happening. I’ll sit down to work on an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting, and I really do intend to follow it. It’s important, and I’m not trying to tune out.\n\nBut then a few minutes in, I realize my mind has gone somewhere else, and I’m rereading the same numbers over and over, or I suddenly notice I’ve missed the point of what someone was saying. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because these are things I should be able to track. I don’t know if that’s the kind of example you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed similar difficulties staying focused in other areas of your life, like at home or during social interactions, or does this mostly happen at work?\n\nPatient: I notice it most at work, I think. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart everywhere, because I’m not, but work is where it’s become hard to ignore.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I’ll start an EPIC quality report or sit down in a clinic metrics meeting with every intention of following it, and then within a few minutes my mind has drifted so far that I’m rereading the same numbers or realizing I missed the whole point of the discussion.\n\nThat’s been embarrassing, because these are important things. I’m not sure if I’ve noticed it as clearly at home or socially, or maybe the consequences just aren’t as obvious there. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you feel down or sad most of the time, even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not most of the time. I mean, I have frustrating days, and I can feel discouraged when I’m not keeping up the way I think I should, but I wouldn’t describe it as being down or sad for no clear reason most of the day.\n\nAt least, I haven’t really noticed a pattern like that. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy, even when you haven’t been doing anything particularly strenuous?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially after a long workday, but I haven’t really noticed feeling wiped out or low-energy when I haven’t done much.\n\nAt least nothing that stood out to me as a pattern. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you often feel overwhelmed by tasks that require planning or organization, or if you find yourself starting projects without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I often start projects without thinking them through. I usually try to be pretty responsible about that, maybe because of my job.\n\nThe planning and organization part… I guess some tasks can feel bigger in my head than they probably should, but I’m not sure I’d call it overwhelmed exactly. It may depend on the task. If there are a lot of pieces to line up, I can feel a little stuck getting started, but I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of times when you’ve acted quickly without thinking things through, like saying something you later regretted or making a decision without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’m sure I’ve had moments where I answered too quickly or wished I’d phrased something differently, like anyone, but I can’t think of a pattern of making impulsive decisions or saying things that cause real problems.\n\nI try to be pretty careful, especially at work. If anything, I probably think things over too much sometimes. Is that helpful, or am I being too narrow with the question?\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you feel less interested in things you usually care about, or where you don’t get as much pleasure out of activities as you normally would?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way that really stands out. I mean, everyone has off days where you’re tired or just not as excited about something, but I haven’t noticed that I’m not enjoying things I normally care about.\n\nAt least, nothing that feels like a pattern. I still care about my usual activities and responsibilities. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situations outside of work, like when you’re reading, watching something, or talking with friends, where you’ve had trouble staying focused or found your mind wandering a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work it can happen, but honestly the thing that pushed me to come in is more at work.\n\nLike, I’ll start an EPIC quality report or sit down in a clinic metrics meeting really meaning to follow everything, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has gone somewhere else entirely. I’m rereading the same numbers, or I suddenly realize I missed the main point of what someone was saying.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m not trying, because I am. It’s just… that drifting happens more than I’d like. Is that along the lines of what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days where you felt down or lost interest in things, even outside of work stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve had stressful stretches where I’m tired or kind of worn down, but I wouldn’t say I felt down for several days or stopped caring about things.\n\nAt least nothing that I’ve noticed as a real period like that. I still generally feel like myself outside of the work stress, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve ever had trouble staying focused during everyday conversations or activities at home, like when you’re reading, watching something, or talking with friends, even when work isn’t involved?\n\nPatient: I mean, it can happen some outside of work, but I don’t know that I’d call it the main issue. What really made me feel like I should talk to someone was at work—like I’ll start an EPIC quality report or sit in a clinic metrics meeting genuinely trying to follow along, and then a few minutes later I realize my mind has gone pretty far off track.\n\nI’ll catch myself rereading the same numbers, or I’ll realize I missed the point of what someone was saying. It’s embarrassing, because I do care and I am trying. But yes, that kind of losing focus happens. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
This past week at home… I’m honestly having a little trouble coming up with a strong example. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.

The more obvious thing, and I know I’ve said this, is still at work—like I’ll start an EPIC quality report or sit in a clinic metrics meeting really meaning to follow it, and then a few minutes later I realize I’ve drifted off and I’m rereading the same numbers or I missed the main point.

If anything, I probably think things over too much sometimes.
